The district court did not accept the objections. On August 10, 2026, Judge Jeffrey Vincent Brown entered an order adopting the magistrate judge's recommendation and a final judgment dismissing the case at the district-court level. The dismissal meant I lost the civil action in that court.

That result does not erase the findings embedded in the adopted R&R. It also does not transform those findings into judgments they were not.

The durable record is more complicated: the court adopted a recommendation that ended my case; the same recommendation expressly said Osteen lied to Judge Tollison; and it concluded that my vehicle-seizure allegations stated a constitutional claim before qualified immunity defeated the damages claim.

Those propositions can coexist. In fact, preserving all of them is the only accurate way to report the decision.

Three days before Judge Brown entered final judgment, I had been sentenced in the separate federal misdemeanor case arising from the Lafayette courthouse arrest.

That August 7 sentencing is the next chapter.


# CHAPTER 38 — SENTENCING

On **Friday, August 7, 2026**, I appeared by video for sentencing in *United States v. Reardon*, the federal misdemeanor case arising from my August 25, 2025 arrest at the John M. Shaw United States Courthouse in Lafayette, Louisiana.

The hearing was public enough that I livestreamed the lead-up to it.

The surviving video begins before I reached the room where I would join the court. I told viewers I was trying to get the hearing link because I had wanted to post it publicly. I was heading to the place that had agreed to host the video connection and was worried about being late for an 11:00 a.m. sentencing.

That place was **Our Daily Bread in Galveston, Texas**.

The transcript captures the practical awkwardness of appearing for federal sentencing from another state. I arrived with only minutes to spare, tried to get situated, and told the people helping me that I had an allocution prepared in large print. Once the connection was live, my attorney, **Dustin Talbot**, asked whether I had the statement printed or ready. I did not have the large-print copy physically in front of me and said I could pull it up on my phone.

The written version now in the archive is twenty-nine large-print pages. Its first page contains delivery notes to myself: stand still, face the judge, do not pace, speak slowly, breathe between paragraphs, and aim for roughly nineteen to twenty minutes.

The livestream shows that the statement was not merely a draft that never reached the court. At approximately **27:50** into the recording, I began delivering it, closely tracking the prepared text.

Before that happened, the court established the remote-hearing record.

Talbot confirmed that I was physically at Our Daily Bread in Galveston. The court stated that the attorneys were also participating by video and that **U.S. Magistrate Judge Thomas P. LeBlanc** was physically in chambers in **Lake Charles, Louisiana**.

The video appearance had been requested because my circumstances made a physical trip to Louisiana an extreme hardship. LeBlanc explained that sentencing could proceed remotely only with my consent. I was placed under oath, and when asked whether I consented to proceed by video, I said yes.

The court then summarized the case. I had been charged by one-count Bill of Information under the federal-property disturbance regulation and, after a bench trial, found guilty. The operative conviction was the subsection (b) obstruction offense described in Chapters 30 and 31.

The government spoke before I did.

Its position was more measured than the version I sometimes gave when describing the case afterward.

The prosecutor acknowledged my military service and acknowledged that I believed I was asserting a legitimate First Amendment right. He said, in substance, that he was not angry at me for holding that belief; he disagreed with me on the law.

The government did **not** ask LeBlanc to send me back to jail.

The prosecutor recognized that the maximum imprisonment for the Class C misdemeanor was thirty days and said he did not believe an additional prison sentence was necessary. Instead, he recommended unsupervised probation. He told the court that a Class C misdemeanor could support as much as five years of probation and argued for **at least three years**, with conditions directed at my conduct around federal courthouses.

That was the recommendation I stood up—or, in this case, sat before a camera—to answer.

My prepared allocution opened by identifying who I believed I was in that proceeding:

**“My name is Matthew Oliver Reardon. I am a United States Marine Corps veteran. I am an independent journalist. I am the founder and publisher of We The People News.”**

I then acknowledged the posture plainly. I was before the court convicted of a petty offense carrying a maximum statutory sentence of thirty days. I said I was not asking LeBlanc at sentencing to overturn the verdict. I wanted to explain why I had been on the courthouse steps, what federal personnel had done before August 25, and why I believed probation with courthouse restrictions would be unjust.

The speech then moved backward two months.

I described the **June 24, 2025** U.S. Marshals Service email concerning me. I quoted the language instructing recipients to report **“any suspicious behavior no matter how small”** and emphasized that the email was distributed after the June 23 courthouse encounter, when no criminal charge had been filed against me.

I discussed the June 27 FOIA request and the Marshals Service response I received on **August 22**, three days before my arrest. I described the District Alert Notice, photographs, identifying information, and the inter-district communications discussed in Chapter 28.

At sentencing I used stronger words than the evidentiary record permits this book to use as settled fact.

I told the court the government had **“surveilled me for protected activity,”** had **“prejudiced the judges of this Court against me,”** and had undertaken a **“campaign.”** Those were my conclusions and my advocacy at sentencing.

The FOIA records prove circulation of information, alert material, identifying data, photographs, and communications among federal security personnel. They do not, standing alone, prove that every recipient surveilled me, that every judge became prejudiced, or that an unlawful coordinated campaign existed.

The distinction is not an effort to soften what I said. It is the reason the full allocution belongs in the evidentiary history while the narrator of this edition has to use more disciplined language.

I then described the August 25 protest.

I told LeBlanc that I had stood outside with a sign containing the words **“Fuck the U.S. Marshals Service.”** I invoked *Cohen v. California* and its famous recognition that the government cannot simply criminalize an offensive four-letter political message because listeners dislike the word.

That part of the allocution was directed at speech.

The conviction, however, was not for the wording on the sign.

As the January written judgment makes clear, the conviction rested on what LeBlanc found I did with signs, my body, and a tripod at the courthouse entrance.

That became the first major contradiction between my sentencing account and the trial court's findings.

In the allocution, I told LeBlanc that Deputy Marshal **Hayden Nugent** told me he would seize my equipment if I did not move it, that I picked it up, began stepping backward away from the door, and was arrested **while complying**.

The January 16 Reasons for Judgment say otherwise.

LeBlanc found that Nugent directed me to move the tripod and camera to the far side of the metal barricades; that I said I was fine where I was; that Nugent warned me I would be arrested; that he gave a three-second countdown; and that I did not move the equipment as ordered before the arrest.

Both records now exist.

The allocution proves the account I put before the judge at sentencing.

The written judgment proves what the trial judge had already found from the evidence.

This book cannot collapse the two into a single version merely because I prefer mine.

I made additional allegations about the arrest that day.

I told the court Nugent slammed me to the ground and damaged my 4K camera, new gimbal, and tripod. I alleged that in the holding area he pressed his knuckles into my chest while I was having difficulty breathing and threatened me with a felony assault-on-a-federal-officer charge after I moved his arm away. I said my heart rate was between 140 and 160 beats per minute by the time paramedics were involved and that I believed medical assistance had been delayed.

Those allegations were part of my publicly delivered allocution.

They were **not findings LeBlanc made at sentencing**.

The equipment also later existed as trial exhibits. For that reason, an earlier formulation saying the equipment was literally “destroyed” is too categorical unless a technical record establishes that it was rendered unusable. My position is that it was damaged and no longer functioned as it had before. That is the formulation this edition uses outside quotations from my sentencing argument.

I then turned to the surveillance-footage dispute.

The prepared allocution called that **“the most significant failure in this entire proceeding.”** I told the court that the government had failed to preserve courthouse lobby surveillance, argued that government cameras could have shown activity my own camera angle could not, and said I believed the missing material would have supported my contention that marshals had gestured for me to approach the building.

I went farther in the speech. I characterized the preservation issue as deliberate concealment of exculpatory evidence and referred to matters I understood from a sealed portion of the trial.

This book will not reproduce the sealed testimony.

I made the argument publicly during sentencing, and the livestream establishes that I made it. But the publication rule for this edition is stricter: sealed testimony stays sealed unless the court later makes it public or a public filing independently establishes the same facts.

Public docket material does establish that surveillance preservation had become a real discovery issue. A November 17 defense filing included a proposed order seeking production of all video and audio recordings of me at the John M. Shaw Courthouse from June 2025 forward and, if the footage no longer existed, a sworn explanation of whether it had been preserved, when it was lost or destroyed, and what preservation efforts followed my September request.

A proposed order is not the same as a signed judicial finding. It proves what the defense was asking the court to compel, not that LeBlanc adopted every proposed finding.

That is how the preservation dispute is treated here.

The allocution then described my transfer after the arrest.

I said I was shackled and taken to **St. Martin Parish Jail** and that when I asked what charge I was being booked on I was told there was only a U.S. Marshals hold. I argued in the speech that transporting and holding me without a new charge was unlawful and even used the word **kidnapping**.

Again, that was my legal accusation, not an adjudication.

The fact that a person is held under federal process without a newly announced local charge does not by itself establish the federal kidnapping offense I invoked. The manuscript preserves what I argued without converting my legal conclusion into a criminal finding against the officers.

From there I returned to the federal communications.

I quoted Magistrate Judge **Carol B. Whitehurst's November 12, 2025 recusal language**, including her statement that an objective and reasonable observer could believe she was predisposed to find me guilty and that avoiding the appearance of impropriety matters to public confidence in the judiciary.

At sentencing I argued that Whitehurst had looked at what the Marshals Service had done and found that the pre-charge communications created an unacceptable appearance of bias.

That formulation also requires qualification.

Whitehurst's recusal is significant. It concerned the appearance surrounding **her own continued participation in the case**. It was not a merits judgment that the Marshals Service had illegally poisoned the entire Western District judiciary or that every recipient of an email was biased against me.

I then cited the government's **October 15 protective-order motion**.

The motion expressly identified discovery categories that included communications between the Marshals Service and federal judges at the John Shaw Courthouse and communications between other courthouse security personnel and the Marshals Service.

During allocution I said the government had asked the court to **conceal** those communications from me.

That was too broad.

The motion sought restrictions on dissemination of sensitive discovery material. It did not seek to prevent defense counsel and me from reviewing all material that the protective order permitted us to use for the case. What the motion proves is that those categories of communications existed in the discovery universe and that the government regarded them as sensitive enough to seek restrictions on broader dissemination.

That is already important without giving the filing a purpose it did not state.

Then the allocution reached the issue that mattered most to me at sentencing: **probation itself**.

The government was asking for years of supervision and restrictions connected to federal courthouses. I had already lived under banishment conditions in Mississippi.

I told LeBlanc that I recognized what I called a **“banishment-style condition”** because I had already experienced one. I discussed the 2022 Lafayette County sentence, the five-year banishment term, the November revocation, and the custody that followed. I said I feared another geographic or courthouse-access condition could become a future **tripwire**.

I also told the court that a son had been born by emergency cesarean section while I was confined and that I had been unable to be there. That statement is preserved here as part of my sworn/public sentencing account. This chapter does not use the allocution alone to add medical or family details elsewhere in the manuscript.

My sentencing request was unusual.

I had already spent approximately thirteen days in custody on the federal matter. The statutory imprisonment maximum was thirty days.

I told the court I preferred a **finite sentence** to years of probation with a courthouse condition.

The delivered speech clarified the request: time served if the court would end the matter there, or, if probation with the access restriction was the alternative, I would rather serve the remaining portion of the thirty-day maximum.

I said:

**“I would rather serve every remaining day of a finite sentence than accept an indefinite condition on my right to walk into a public building and do my job.”**

That sentence captures why the government's recommendation felt more threatening to me than additional jail time.

A jail sentence has an end date.

Probation creates continuing jurisdiction and the possibility of a later revocation proceeding.

My Mississippi history made that possibility concrete rather than theoretical in my mind.

But even there the legal description needs precision. A probation condition is not a self-executing trap that automatically sends someone to prison the moment an officer alleges a violation. Revocation still requires process and a judicial determination. My fear was based on how I believed prior revocation proceedings had operated, not on a rule that every accusation becomes automatic incarceration.

The allocution closed with a statement that tied the sentencing hearing directly to this manuscript.

I told LeBlanc I was writing a book about the BOLO alerts, the emails, the arrest, the force allegations, the surveillance dispute, the holding cell, the jail, the protective order, the recusal, and the conviction.

**“I do not say that as a threat,”** I said. **“I say it as a fact. I am a journalist. This is what I do.”**

I also made my objection to probation explicit. I said I did not consent to a condition that I believed restricted my constitutional rights and wanted any such restriction imposed over my express objection rather than characterized as an agreement I had voluntarily accepted.

The final lines were not subtle.

I said I was a Marine and a journalist, that I intended to return to work after serving whatever sentence the court imposed, and thanked the judge.

Then LeBlanc sentenced me.

At approximately **57:44** on the livestream, the court initially described the sentence as time already served plus **two years of unsupervised probation**.

The standard conditions included that I commit no new federal, state, or local crime; not unlawfully possess a controlled substance; and not possess firearms, ammunition, destructive devices, or dangerous weapons. The court suspended the drug-testing requirement because the information before it did not suggest a significant drug-abuse risk.

The special courthouse condition was broader geographically than the John M. Shaw building alone but narrower than a nationwide ban.

LeBlanc ordered that I not be physically present at the **John M. Shaw Federal Courthouse in Lafayette**, any other federal courthouse **within Louisiana**, or adjacent federally owned or leased grounds unless I had **legitimate business** with an occupant of the courthouse. If I did have such business, I was required to notify the U.S. Marshals Service in that locality at least **two business days in advance**.

LeBlanc expressly said the sentence was not intended to suppress my First Amendment activity. He framed it as a response to the conduct for which I had been convicted on federal courthouse property.

Then the hearing became procedurally unusual.

After a breakout discussion requested by the government, the participants returned to the record and LeBlanc clarified that his earlier wording could be misunderstood.

The final structure was **not** “time served” as one completed punishment **plus** two years of probation as a second punishment.

Instead, I was being placed on **two years of unsupervised probation**. The approximately thirteen days I had already spent in custody would operate as credit if probation were later revoked and I were resentenced on the original offense.

That clarification matters enough that the source notes and the manuscript both need to use it consistently. A shorthand description of “time served + two years probation” is incomplete after the judge's clarification.

I immediately saw the problem I had been trying to avoid.

I pressed LeBlanc for a yes-or-no answer to whether I could ever spend more than thirty total days in custody on the original petty offense if probation were later revoked.

The judge declined to answer the hypothetical and directed me to counsel. The prosecutor explained that future circumstances could involve different legal permutations, including potentially new charges, making the categorical question unsuitable for an on-the-spot ruling.

The hearing therefore does **not** contain a judicial holding answering every future revocation scenario.

It contains something more limited:

I was sentenced to two years of unsupervised probation.

If the court later revoked that probation and resentenced me on the original offense, the custody already served would be credited.

LeBlanc did not give me the broader advisory ruling I wanted about all possible future custody.

The court imposed **no fine**.

LeBlanc also **waived the special assessment**, explaining that the record did not show I was financially able to pay a fine or assessment.

Talbot then put an objection to the special courthouse condition on the record.

He argued that the phrase **“legitimate business”** was vague. The government responded that I could do what similarly situated members of the public could do so long as I complied with the rules, standing orders, and federal regulations governing the courthouse.

LeBlanc overruled the objection. He found the reference to legitimate business with courthouse tenants or occupants sufficiently clear.

I disagree with that ruling and have treated the condition as an appellate issue. But that is the procedural posture: objection preserved, government response made, objection overruled.

Near the end of the hearing, LeBlanc advised me of the appeal route.

I had **fourteen days after entry of judgment** to file a written notice of appeal. Because the petty-offense case had been tried and sentenced by a magistrate judge, the first appeal was to a **United States district judge** under Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 58(g)(2), not directly to the Fifth Circuit.

Earlier drafts that described a direct Fifth Circuit appeal were wrong.

The hearing ended after approximately an hour and a half. LeBlanc said the court would prepare the written judgment, concluded the sentencing, and wished me good luck.

The most revealing thing about the hearing is not that either side obtained everything it asked for.

The government requested at least three years of unsupervised probation. LeBlanc imposed two.

I asked for time served with no probation—or, failing that, said I preferred the remaining days of the thirty-day imprisonment ceiling. The court rejected that choice and imposed probation.

I objected to the courthouse condition. The objection was preserved but overruled.

And I put into the public record a long argument about federal information sharing, preservation of evidence, the Marshals Service, Whitehurst's recusal, my Mississippi history, and why I believed the prosecution fit a larger pattern.

Some of those arguments remain central to how I understand what happened.

Some were stated too categorically in the allocution and have been narrowed in this edition because the underlying documents do not prove the conclusion as strongly as I told the sentencing court they did.

That difference is not something I want hidden from the reader.

The August 7 allocution is evidence of my position at that moment.

The court's judgments and orders are evidence of what the judiciary actually found.

The FOIA records are evidence of what federal personnel actually wrote and distributed.

The videos are evidence of what they show.

And where those categories conflict, the conflict stays visible.

That is the standard I am applying to this book even when it cuts against the story I once told about myself.

Three days after sentencing, in the separate Texas civil case, Judge Jeffrey Brown entered the order and final judgment discussed in the previous chapter.

The legal fights were narrowing.

The financial consequences were not.


# CHAPTER 39 — THE FINANCIAL COST OF THE FIGHT

By the time of the August 2026 sentencing, the disputes described in this book had moved through local, state, and federal systems in Mississippi, Texas, Utah, and Louisiana. The records document arrests, detention, bonds, towing, filing costs, travel, and years of litigation. They do not reduce neatly to one dollar figure, and I am not going to manufacture one.

Some amounts can be stated precisely.

After the May 26, 2017 aggravated-stalking arrest, the Oxford Eagle reported that Lafayette County Justice Court Judge Carolyn Bell set bond at $150,000. The article said release required ten percent—$15,000—plus collateral for the balance. That is a contemporaneous published report; until the underlying bond order is placed in the evidence archive, I treat the amount as reported rather than as a court-order quotation.

The Galveston felony DWI case produced another documented bond problem. A March 7, 2024 motion filed by my attorney, Myron G. Davis, states that the bond in Cause No. 23-CR-2981 was then $100,000 and had previously been $10,000. The motion sought a reduction. The request was denied. Whatever theory one applies to bond generally, the practical effect in my case was continued detention because I could not post the amount required for release.

The DWI charge was later dismissed on May 24, 2024. That dismissal should not be rewritten as a reduction to a lesser offense; it was a dismissal. Nor should the toxicology result be turned into a complete explanation for why the prosecutor dismissed the case unless the dismissal record itself says so. The laboratory evidence and the disposition are both important. They are separate facts.

The smaller numbers mattered too.

In June 2026, after Galveston officers arrested me in a separate incident and my 2016 Mitsubishi Outlander was towed, my records reflect a $373.94 charge associated with approximately two days at Marty's City Auto. Several hundred dollars is small beside a six-figure bond. For a person relying on a vehicle for transportation and, at times, shelter, it can be the amount that decides whether the vehicle comes back at all.

That is where the financial story becomes harder to quantify without becoming less real.

I have said publicly that I lost four houses over a period of years while these cases and disputes were consuming my time and money. That is my account. I have not assembled deeds, mortgage records, foreclosure records, sale documents, or a property-by-property financial ledger proving the cause and timing of each loss. This book therefore does not state that a particular official or lawsuit caused the loss of four houses as an independently proven fact.

What the record does establish is prolonged interruption of ordinary life. I spent extended periods in custody. I traveled repeatedly between jurisdictions. I filed and defended cases without counsel for substantial stretches. I paid or faced filing fees, records fees, bond requirements, transportation costs, towing charges, and the ordinary costs of trying to maintain work and housing while litigation continued.

The difference between having counsel and proceeding alone also appears repeatedly in the record, but an earlier version of this chapter overstated the lesson.

My 2022 federal civil-rights case in the Northern District of Mississippi was dismissed with prejudice. In 2024, Jacob Howard of the MacArthur Justice Center filed a post-conviction petition attacking the July 18, 2024 revocation sentence. That representation led to the January 2025 order correcting the illegal revocation sentence and to my release the following day.

It did not produce a full vacatur of the underlying 2022 aggravated-stalking conviction. Earlier drafts said it did. They were wrong.

The contrast between those cases still shows why legal representation matters, but it does not prove that counsel alone determines outcomes or that the two cases involved identical claims. They did not. Different causes of action, procedural postures, records, standards, and remedies were involved. What I can say from experience is simpler: trained counsel can identify procedural routes and legal arguments that are difficult for a pro se litigant to discover while simultaneously living through the events being litigated.

My own filings became more sophisticated over time because I spent years reading rules, cases, statutes, transcripts, and dockets. That education was not free. It consumed time that otherwise could have gone to ordinary employment, family life, reporting, or rebuilding financial stability. Government defendants, by contrast, ordinarily appear through salaried public lawyers or retained counsel paid by the governmental entity or insurer. That institutional difference is structural; it does not require proof that anyone deliberately set out to bankrupt a litigant.

That is why I am narrowing the title's older phrase, "financial warfare," into something I can prove more cleanly: financial cost.

I cannot show that every fee, bond, tow, or delay was imposed for the purpose of exhausting me financially. I can show that the costs accumulated. I can show that detention prevented work. I can show that high bonds kept me in custody when I could not post them. I can show towing and records charges in actual documents. And I can show years in which I was simultaneously trying to litigate, report, travel, work, and maintain basic stability.

For an institution, litigation is an operational expense. For an individual, litigation can become the operating environment of an entire life.

That is the economic fact this record supports without needing a conspiracy theory to explain it.

The epilogue does not end with every case resolved. It ends with a record much cleaner than the one I started with: victories described as victories, losses described as losses, allegations separated from findings, and contradictions corrected where the documents require it.


# EPILOGUE — THE RECORD

This book began with a 2016 flag petition and followed what happened afterward through arrests, family-court litigation, criminal prosecutions, civil commitments, public-records fights, federal lawsuits, interstate detention, and appellate proceedings in Mississippi, Texas, Utah, and Louisiana.

The ending is not a verdict that makes every event line up on one side.

It is a record.

On August 7, 2026, Judge Thomas P. LeBlanc sentenced me in the Lafayette federal-courthouse case. After clarifying the sentence on the record, the court placed me on two years of unsupervised probation. Time I had already served would be credited if the probation were later revoked and resentencing occurred. The court imposed no fine and waived the special assessment. It also prohibited my presence at the John M. Shaw Federal Courthouse, other federal courthouses in Louisiana, and adjacent federally owned or leased grounds unless I had legitimate business with a courthouse occupant and gave the U.S. Marshals Service two business days' notice.

My attorney objected that the "legitimate business" condition was vague. The court overruled the objection. The judge also told me that the sentence was not intended to suppress my First Amendment activity and advised me of my right to appeal the conviction and sentence to a United States district judge after entry of judgment.

Three days later, on August 10, 2026, Judge Jeffrey V. Brown adopted Magistrate Judge Andrew M. Edison's recommendation in Reardon v. Osteen and entered final judgment against me in the Texas federal civil-rights case.

I lost that case at the district-court level.

The loss does not erase what the adopted recommendation said. Edison wrote that "the record shows that Osteen lied to Judge Tollison" and called that fact disturbing. The same recommendation concluded that my allegations concerning the later vehicle impoundment stated an unreasonable-seizure claim before recommending qualified immunity. It also rejected my effort to characterize Jeff Busby's affidavit as literally false on the theory I had advanced, relying on evidence that Assistant District Attorney Steven Jubera—not Busby—personally transmitted the March 2024 letter to Texas.

That is what this manuscript has become by the end: not a brief that keeps only the helpful rulings, but a record that keeps the adverse ones too.

The January 2025 post-conviction order did not vacate my underlying 2022 aggravated-stalking conviction. It corrected the July 2024 revocation sentence and led to my release on January 22. The February 24, 2026 Mississippi Court of Appeals decision did not acquit me of the Lafayette County Justice Court misdemeanors. It reversed the Circuit Court's dismissal of my appeal and remanded the matter so the appeal could proceed. The appellate clerk's own records show that Lafayette County Circuit Clerk Jeff Busby had earlier received a record-delinquency notice and then a show-cause notice while that appeal was being assembled.

Those distinctions matter because the strongest version of this story is not the version in which every setback becomes a vindication and every inconsistency becomes corruption.

The strongest version is the one the documents can survive.

The documents establish that the Criminal Information in the 2017 aggravated-stalking case alleged AR-15 conduct "on or before May 8, 2017," while the bill of sale in my records shows that I acquired the rifle on May 20. They establish that the precise May 8 allegation appears in the later charging instrument and plea record; they do not, from the material currently located, establish that the same date appeared in the pre-arrest warrant paperwork used on May 26. That difference is now preserved rather than blurred.

The documents establish that Oxford Police referred the June 20, 2022 City Hall incident to the Mississippi Bureau of Investigation on June 23, that Agent Joey Mauney obtained the arrest warrant on June 27, and that deputies arrested me at the Chancery Court the following morning because they knew I had court there. The Chancery Court was the arrest location, not the conduct that generated the warrant.

The documents establish that the Galveston toxicology testing did not show alcohol and that the DWI prosecution was dismissed on May 24, 2024. They also establish that a federal magistrate later wrote that Osteen lied to Judge Tollison. They do not permit me to call that finding a criminal conviction for perjury, and this book no longer does.

The documents establish that the U.S. Marshals Service was circulating information about me in June 2025, including material labeling me a First Amendment auditor, before the August 25 Lafayette arrest. They do not establish that the BOLO itself caused that arrest. The January 2026 federal judgment separately found that on August 25 I blocked courthouse doors and refused an instruction to move my tripod before I was arrested. Both records belong in the same book.

The documents establish that the New Orleans RTCC camera did not capture the physical altercation at City Hall. They do not establish that the footage was deliberately lost. They establish that New Orleans later withheld requested RTCC and 911 material under a pending-criminal-litigation exemption while offering an initial report for a fee. That is different from proving that the City created the criminal case in order to hide the evidence.

The documents also forced corrections to me.

At the Mississippi Governor's Mansion in July 2025, the contemporaneous recording has me telling the supervisor that the trooper did not physically touch me. An earlier manuscript said he struck me, knocked down my phone, and stepped on it. That version is gone.

At sentencing in August 2026, I told the court that I moved my courthouse equipment when Nugent ordered me to do so and was arrested while complying. The written trial findings say I refused, was warned, heard a countdown, and was arrested after failing to move it. This book reports that conflict instead of quietly choosing the version most useful to me.

I also described the government's October 2025 protective-order motion too broadly in public. The filing sought restrictions on dissemination of sensitive discovery, including Marshal–judge communications. It did not itself prove an effort to conceal misconduct. That correction is now part of the manuscript as well.

That is what accountability has to mean if I am going to demand it from anyone else.

I remain the narrator of this book. I am also an interested party in much of the litigation it describes. Those facts make source discipline more important, not less. Memory can explain why an event mattered to me. It cannot be allowed to override a transcript, a signed order, a laboratory report, or a contemporaneous recording when the two disagree.

There are still unresolved matters in this record. Some cases remain subject to further review or proceedings. Some documents have not been located. Some recordings still need direct verification before quotations should be treated as publication-grade. Where the evidence stops, this book stops with it.

I chose the title American Injustice because the question running through these pages is larger than whether every accusation I made was right. The question is what happens when a citizen keeps demanding records, hearings, review, and accountability from institutions that possess far more money, legal expertise, and coercive power than he does—and what those same institutions owe him even when he is angry, profane, difficult, or wrong about part of his own case.

The Constitution does not require a citizen to be likable before the government has to obey it.

It also does not excuse a citizen from telling the truth about his own conduct.

That is the standard I am leaving on the page.


# LEGAL ANALYSIS

*A note on what this section is. This is an explanation of the legal doctrines that shaped the events described in the manuscript. It is written for a reader, not as a brief and not as legal advice. Where a doctrine cuts against my position, I say so. Where the law remains unsettled or the record is incomplete, I do not convert uncertainty into a holding.*

---

**I. RETALIATORY ARREST: PROBABLE CAUSE, NIEVES, AND GONZALEZ**

A central allegation running through this book is retaliation: that protected speech, recording, reporting, and criticism influenced decisions to investigate, arrest, charge, or restrict me. Federal law makes that kind of claim difficult even when motive evidence exists.

In *Nieves v. Bartlett*, 587 U.S. 391 (2019), the Supreme Court held that a retaliatory-arrest plaintiff generally must plead and prove the absence of probable cause. The Court recognized a narrow exception where objective evidence shows that the plaintiff was arrested while otherwise similarly situated people not engaged in the protected speech were not.

That exception was clarified in *Gonzalez v. Trevino*, 602 U.S. 653 (2024). The Supreme Court rejected the Fifth Circuit's demand for virtually identical, individually identified comparators. Objective evidence can take other forms. In *Gonzalez*, a survey showing that a longstanding criminal statute had never been used for comparable conduct was the kind of evidence a court could consider.

That matters to my own civil-rights litigation. The lesson is not simply that I needed a list of named people who did the exact same thing and were not arrested. The stronger lesson is that retaliation claims need objective proof of differential enforcement. Motive evidence may matter, but under *Nieves* it often does not carry the claim by itself when probable cause exists.

**II. QUALIFIED IMMUNITY: A CLAIM THAT SURVIVED ONE STEP AND STILL LOST**

Earlier versions of this book said I "won" a Fourth Amendment seizure claim on the merits in *Reardon v. Osteen*. That overstated the ruling.

Magistrate Judge Andrew Edison wrote that because impounding my vehicle was unreasonable on the facts alleged, I had **stated a constitutional claim for unreasonable seizure**. That was a pleading-stage determination in a Report and Recommendation addressing motions to dismiss. It was not a final merits judgment and it did not establish damages liability.

Edison nevertheless recommended qualified immunity because I had not carried the separate burden of identifying clearly established law sufficient to overcome the defense. Judge Jeffrey V. Brown later adopted the recommendation and entered final judgment against me.

Qualified immunity therefore creates two distinct questions: whether the alleged conduct states a constitutional violation, and whether the right was clearly established with sufficient specificity at the time of the conduct. A plaintiff can clear the first question and lose on the second. In my case, the court concluded that my pleading cleared the constitutional-claim threshold on the impoundment issue but did not overcome qualified immunity.

My objections invoked *Hope v. Pelzer*, 536 U.S. 730 (2002), and its warning that officials can have fair notice even without a prior case containing identical facts. The district court adopted the R&R despite those objections. Whatever I may argue on review, the publication record must describe the actual result: I did not obtain a merits judgment in my favor.

**III. WHY AN EARLY PRO SE §1983 CASE FAILED**

One of my earlier federal cases arising from Olive Branch was dismissed for failure to state a claim. I have sometimes folded that dismissal into a broader story of institutional resistance. The pleading itself shows more ordinary legal problems.

Section 1983 does not provide damages against a state simply because a state actor is alleged to have violated federal rights. States generally retain sovereign immunity from federal damages suits absent waiver or valid congressional abrogation, and a State is not a "person" for ordinary §1983 damages purposes. Prospective relief against an appropriate state official presents a different question, but that was not how I framed the case.

Municipal liability presents another barrier. Under *Monell v. Department of Social Services*, 436 U.S. 658 (1978), a city is not automatically liable under §1983 for every constitutional wrong committed by an employee. The plaintiff must connect the violation to a municipal policy, custom, or qualifying decision by a final policymaker. My early pleading did not do that adequately.

I also used a "federal corporation" theory that had no sound basis in §1983 doctrine. That language weakened the pleading instead of helping it.

Those defects matter because they distinguish a lost constitutional claim from a complaint that never pleaded the required legal elements. A fair account of my litigation has to admit when procedure and pleading—not a factual finding against me—ended the case.

**IV. JUDICIAL IMMUNITY AND THE OLIVE BRANCH CONTEMPT ARREST**

Judges generally have absolute immunity from damages for judicial acts performed within their jurisdiction, even where the act is alleged to have been erroneous, malicious, or in excess of authority. *Stump v. Sparkman*, 435 U.S. 349 (1978); *Mireles v. Waco*, 502 U.S. 9 (1991). Immunity does not extend to nonjudicial acts or acts taken in the complete absence of jurisdiction.

That doctrine makes a damages claim arising directly from a courtroom contempt ruling exceptionally difficult. The stronger constitutional issue in my Olive Branch episode concerned impartiality. *In re Murchison*, 349 U.S. 133 (1955), and *Mayberry v. Pennsylvania*, 400 U.S. 455 (1971), recognize that due process can require a different judge when the presiding judge has become personally embroiled in the contempt controversy.

The contempt charge against me was later dismissed. That disposition does not itself establish civil liability, and judicial immunity does not disappear because a charge was dismissed. The episode therefore illustrates a distinction repeated throughout this book: conduct can be subject to serious constitutional criticism while a damages remedy remains unavailable.

**V. THE GALVESTON BLOOD WARRANT: THE ISSUE IS VALIDITY, NOT WHETHER A WARRANT EXISTED**

Earlier drafts treated the August 2023 blood draw as though the existence of a warrant were unresolved. The present record does not support that framing.

Osteen signed a blood-warrant affidavit. The case record reflects that a magistrate signed a warrant at approximately 2:49 a.m., and Osteen later testified under oath that he filed a blood warrant that was signed by a judge. I was not shown the warrant at the hospital, but failure to hand me a copy at the moment of the draw is not the same thing as absence of a warrant.

The legal dispute is therefore closer to *Franks v. Delaware*, 438 U.S. 154 (1978): whether an affidavit contained deliberately or recklessly false material statements or omissions, and, if so, whether the corrected affidavit would still establish probable cause.

The contradictions are documented. Osteen's affidavit marked a moderate odor of alcohol on my breath and described dilated pupils. Later testimony said he could not smell alcohol coming from me once I was outside the vehicle and described my pupils as very small or pinpoint. The laboratory reported **no alcohol detected**. At the July 2024 Mississippi hearing, Osteen nevertheless testified that alcohol had been detected. The same laboratory production separately reported methamphetamine at 0.13 mg/L. No expert testimony at that revocation hearing established that the reported concentration caused impairment.

My attorney later sought a *Franks* hearing in the Texas misdemeanor case. That hearing was not held. In the subsequent federal civil case, Edison concluded that probable cause remained even after addressing disputed portions of the affidavit, but he also wrote that "the record shows that Osteen lied to Judge Tollison." Those are separate propositions. The federal court's use of the word *lied* is not a criminal adjudication of perjury, and the absence of alcohol does not erase the separate methamphetamine result.

The publication-safe legal conclusion is therefore narrow: the blood draw was warrant-based; the affidavit and later testimony contain documented contradictions; materiality was contested; and no Texas *Franks* hearing resolved that dispute before the criminal matter ended.

**VI. THE TEXAS FELONY ENHANCEMENT AND ITS DISMISSAL**

The Texas indictment charged DWI third or more and alleged two prior offenses. That enhancement therefore did not appear from nowhere. The dispute was whether the alleged priors could legally support the felony level.

One of the alleged predicates was the Lafayette County misdemeanor DUI conviction that I had been trying to appeal since December 2021. The later MacArthur Justice Center PCR petition cited Texas authority for the proposition that an appealed prior conviction used for enhancement does not become final until the appellate mandate issues. The same petition attached the Texas motion to dismiss and described the State's stated reason as that it "cannot prove up jurisdictional enhance[e]ments."

The felony prosecution in Cause No. 23-CR-2981 was dismissed without prejudice on May 24, 2024. The later record reflects that a misdemeanor DWI matter arising from the same August 11 stop remained pending and became the vehicle for the February 2025 *Franks* motion.

That chronology is stronger than the old rhetoric. I do not need to call the felony a fabricated enhancement or infer why prosecutors acted beyond what their filing says. The documentary point is that Texas indicted the felony, later moved to dismiss because it could not prove the jurisdictional enhancements, and the felony case was dismissed.

**VII. THE 2017 COVENANT NOT TO SUE**

The signed covenant is not missing. It is in the appellate record for LK17-295. It states that, as a specific condition of the guilty plea, I agreed not to file civil claims against Lafayette County, the sheriff and deputies, the City of Oxford and its officials, and Todd and Ashley Lynch for matters through the arrest.

Release-dismissal agreements are not automatically unenforceable. *Town of Newton v. Rumery*, 480 U.S. 386 (1987), requires a case-specific inquiry that includes voluntariness, prosecutorial overreaching, and the public interest.

That is the proper framework for the facts I describe: a $150,000 bond, a negotiated guilty plea, banishment, and a covenant signed as part of the disposition. My later account is that I accepted the bargain under intense pressure. The plea record also contains my sworn answers at the time denying coercion and acknowledging the rights I was giving up. Both pieces belong in any honest analysis.

The book cannot adjudicate whether the covenant would be enforceable in a later civil action. It can document that criminal disposition and civil release were linked in the same bargain and identify the doctrine that would govern a challenge.

**VIII. NOTICE AND ACCESS TO THE ACCUSATION**

The Sixth Amendment guarantees a criminal defendant the right to be informed of the nature and cause of the accusation. How that right applies depends on the stage of the case and the documents at issue.

Several episodes in this manuscript involve my complaint that I could not obtain the document being used against me when I believed I needed it: the initiating paperwork in the 2017 Lafayette case; charging material in the 2020 Olive Branch matter; and affidavit-related filings in Galveston. The records do not support collapsing those episodes into a single judicial finding that three courts denied notice in the same legal sense.

The stronger way to present the pattern is evidentiary rather than conclusory: identify what I requested, when I requested it, what the court or agency produced, and what remained unavailable. A reader can then see the recurring access problem without the manuscript declaring a constitutional violation that no court decided.

**IX. MISSISSIPPI REVOCATION PROCESS: 72 HOURS, 21 DAYS, 30 DAYS, AND TECHNICAL VIOLATIONS**

*Morrissey v. Brewer*, 408 U.S. 471 (1972), established minimum due-process protections for parole revocation, and *Gagnon v. Scarpelli*, 411 U.S. 778 (1973), extended that framework to probation.

Mississippi now states important timing rules directly in Miss. Code Ann. § 47-7-37. For an offender arrested on a probation-violation warrant, the Department must ordinarily hold an informal preliminary hearing within seventy-two hours unless an exception or waiver applies. If reasonable cause is found, detention pending the revocation hearing is generally limited to twenty-one days; if the hearing is not held in that period, the statute directs release back to probation status. A separate subsection provides that the revocation charge shall be dismissed if the revocation hearing is not held within thirty days of the warrant's issuance unless good cause for delay is established in the record.

The same statute caps incarceration for technical violations: up to ninety days for a first technical revocation, 120 days for a second, 180 days or the remainder for a third, and up to the remainder for a fourth or later revocation.

Those distinctions correct one of the most important errors in earlier editions. The January 21, 2025 post-conviction order did **not** vacate my underlying 2022 aggravated-stalking conviction. It vacated the July 2024 two-year revocation sentence, treated the violation as technical, resentenced me to 120 days, ordered my immediate release because I had served more than that, and reinstated the original unsupervised probation and banishment terms. The MacArthur petition separately argued that the earlier November 2022 one-year technical-revocation sentence exceeded the statutory first-revocation cap, but that already-served sentence was not the relief granted by the January 2025 order.

The timing issues surrounding my 2024 detention still require exact arrest, warrant, continuance, and hearing dates. The statute supplies the clocks. The record supplies the dates. The manuscript should not invent tolling rules or call a violation established until those two are matched precisely.

**X. MISSING OR UNPRESERVED EVIDENCE**

The law distinguishes evidence that is exculpatory from evidence that is merely potentially useful.

*Brady v. Maryland*, 373 U.S. 83 (1963), concerns suppression of material exculpatory evidence in the government's possession. *California v. Trombetta*, 467 U.S. 479 (1984), addresses evidence whose exculpatory value was apparent before destruction. *Arizona v. Youngblood*, 488 U.S. 51 (1988), generally requires a showing of bad faith when the State fails to preserve only potentially useful evidence.

That framework matters because missing video cannot honestly be described as proof of what the video would have shown. The December 2020 security-camera dispute, the Lafayette sallyport/dash-camera questions, the New Orleans RTCC camera that did not capture the City Hall altercation, and the federal courthouse preservation issue are not interchangeable.

The safe rule for this book is simple: if a camera did not cover the event, say so; if footage once existed but was not preserved, say that; if a request produced only part of what was sought, identify what was produced; and never write the contents of footage that nobody has seen.

**XI. THE RIGHT TO RECORD AND ITS LIMITS**

In *Turner v. Driver*, 848 F.3d 678 (5th Cir. 2017), the Fifth Circuit recognized a First Amendment right to record police officers performing official duties in public, subject to reasonable time, place, and manner restrictions. That decision predates the May 2017 events in this book and governs Mississippi, Louisiana, and Texas.

The scope matters. *Turner* does not establish an unlimited right to film anywhere government business occurs. It does not eliminate neutral security rules, obstruction laws, restricted areas, or reasonable facility regulations. Recording a police officer on a public sidewalk and placing equipment in a federal courthouse entrance present different legal questions.

That distinction is especially important in the 2025 federal courthouse case. My First Amendment activity formed part of the factual context, but the conviction was for conduct under 41 C.F.R. § 102-74.390(b). The trial court found prohibited conduct at the courthouse. Whether that conviction survives review is a separate question from the general existence of a right to record police in public.

**XII. COMMUNICARE AND THE STATE-ACTION QUESTION**

Section 1983 reaches conduct under color of state law. Private entities do not become state actors merely because they participate in a heavily regulated field or use a state-created procedure.

Cases such as *Lugar v. Edmondson Oil Co.*, 457 U.S. 922 (1982), and *West v. Atkins*, 487 U.S. 42 (1988), show that state action can exist where private conduct is fairly attributable to the State—through joint participation, delegation of a public function, or other sufficiently close connection. But the inquiry is fact-specific.

My commitment history involved affidavits, chancery-court process, and execution of writs by law enforcement. Rachel Alcorn also told me in a recorded call that Communicare was not a county agency and described its relationship to the Mississippi Department of Mental Health. Those facts do not by themselves answer whether Communicare or a particular employee acted under color of state law for a particular act.

That question remains unresolved in the present source archive. The publication version therefore should not label Communicare a government agency or announce that §1983 liability is established. It should document what each actor did and leave the legal classification where the evidence leaves it: open.

**XIII. CIVIL COMMITMENT AND DUE PROCESS**

Civil commitment is not criminal punishment, but it is a serious deprivation of liberty. *O'Connor v. Donaldson*, 422 U.S. 563 (1975), limits confinement of a nondangerous person capable of living safely in freedom. *Addington v. Texas*, 441 U.S. 418 (1979), requires clear and convincing evidence for civil commitment. Other cases recognize procedural protections appropriate to the nature of the confinement.

My December 7, 2021 recording documents a narrower issue: the deputy serving the writ told me he did not have the accompanying affidavit and the paperwork in hand did not identify who had signed it. Later court records show that an affidavit did exist.

That evidence supports saying the service packet I received did not include the affidavit. It does **not**, without a statute or controlling case requiring contemporaneous service of that affidavit, establish by itself that the seizure was unconstitutional. Earlier drafts crossed that line. This version does not.

**XIV. FAVORABLE TERMINATION AFTER THOMPSON v. CLARK**

*Thompson v. Clark*, 596 U.S. 36 (2022), held that for the favorable-termination element of the Fourth Amendment claim recognized there, a plaintiff need show that the criminal prosecution ended without a conviction; an affirmative indication of innocence is not required.

That rule can matter to proceedings in this book that actually terminated without a conviction—for example, a charge that was dismissed or refused. It does not convert every procedural success into favorable termination.

The February 24, 2026 decision in *Reardon v. State* is a good example. The Mississippi Court of Appeals reversed the Circuit Court's dismissal of my misdemeanor appeal and remanded. It did **not** reverse the underlying Justice Court convictions. Likewise, the January 2025 PCR order corrected a revocation sentence; it did not terminate the underlying 2022 stalking conviction.

A remand, a sentence correction, and a dismissal are legally different dispositions. The manuscript now keeps them separate.

**XV. PROSECUTORIAL IMMUNITY**

Under *Imbler v. Pachtman*, 424 U.S. 409 (1976), prosecutors generally have absolute immunity from §1983 damages for advocative conduct intimately associated with the judicial phase of a prosecution. *Buckley v. Fitzsimmons*, 509 U.S. 259 (1993), distinguishes investigative or administrative functions, and *Kalina v. Fletcher*, 522 U.S. 118 (1997), recognizes that personally swearing to facts can place a prosecutor in the role of a complaining witness rather than an advocate.

That functional distinction matters more than the prosecutor's title. Filing and arguing a motion to revoke are ordinarily advocative acts. Investigating facts before probable cause, administering policy, or personally attesting to facts can present different questions.

The careful conclusion is not that prosecutors are categorically beyond accountability. It is that §1983 damages are generally unavailable for core prosecutorial advocacy, even when the plaintiff alleges serious misconduct in presenting the case.

**XVI. BANISHMENT AS A SENTENCING CONDITION**

The 2022 sentencing order imposed five years of unsupervised probation and banishment from Lafayette County, subject to stated exceptions. I accepted that condition in a negotiated plea.

Banishment conditions are not automatically unconstitutional, and Mississippi has permitted them in appropriate circumstances. Their validity can depend on the relationship between the condition and legitimate sentencing purposes, the scope of the restriction, and the record supporting it.

The November 2022 revocation transcript adds an important factual limit to my own argument. Deputy Beavers confirmed that I told the Sheriff's Department in advance that I intended to go to the FBI office. Advance notice is documented. But the order did not say that notice alone automatically authorized entry into Lafayette County for any purpose. Judge Tollison found that my presence fell outside the permitted purposes and revoked the suspended sentence.

I continue to dispute that outcome and the evidence used to reach it. The manuscript no longer claims that the phone call itself conclusively established legal authorization under the order.

**XVII. PRESERVATION, SPOLIATION, AND WHAT A MISSING RECORD PROVES**

The word *spoliation* can hide several different legal problems. Criminal due process, civil discovery sanctions, evidentiary inferences, and a court's inherent authority do not all use the same test.

In the criminal context, *Trombetta* and *Youngblood* set demanding standards for destroyed or unpreserved evidence. In civil litigation, rules governing discovery and sanctions can operate differently. And sometimes the factual answer is simpler: a requested camera never recorded the relevant area in the first place.

That last point changed the New Orleans chapters. The RTCC record did not establish that footage of the actual altercation was destroyed. It established that the camera produced did not capture the altercation itself. Calling that "lost footage" would create evidence that the source does not contain.

The same discipline applies everywhere else: absence of evidence may matter, but it does not supply the missing evidence's contents.

**XVIII. SPEEDY TRIAL AND REVOCATION DEADLINES ARE DIFFERENT QUESTIONS**

Constitutional speedy-trial claims are governed by *Barker v. Wingo*, 407 U.S. 514 (1972), which balances length of delay, reason for delay, assertion of the right, and prejudice. Pretrial incarceration is one recognized form of prejudice, along with anxiety and impairment of the defense. Delay attributable to the defense also matters in the balance.

Mississippi's 72-hour, 21-day, and 30-day revocation rules are statutory deadlines addressing probation-revocation procedure. They should not be blended into a *Barker* analysis merely because both concern delay.

Earlier versions of my arguments sometimes mixed those frameworks. The corrected manuscript keeps them separate: *Barker* for constitutional speedy-trial analysis; § 47-7-37 for Mississippi probation-revocation timing; and the actual docket for which party requested which continuance.

**XIX. WHAT THE RECORD TAUGHT ME ABOUT CIVIL-RIGHTS LITIGATION**

The clearest lessons from my own cases are narrower than the conclusions I once drew.

First, motive evidence is not enough by itself in many retaliatory-arrest cases. Objective enforcement evidence matters, especially after *Nieves* and *Gonzalez*.

Second, stating a constitutional claim is not the same as obtaining a judgment that a constitutional violation occurred, and neither is the same as overcoming qualified immunity.

Third, immunities and pleading rules can end civil-rights cases without a trial on the underlying factual dispute.

Fourth, precise documents are stronger than grand theories. A signed order, a transcript line, a laboratory report, a docket notice, or a dated recording can survive adversarial review in a way an inferred motive often cannot.

Fifth, favorable termination, reversal, remand, vacatur of a sentence, vacatur of a conviction, and dismissal are not synonyms. This manuscript once used some of them as if they were. It no longer does.

Sixth, many of my pro se losses turned on procedure, jurisdiction, immunity, or pleading sufficiency. That does not prove the underlying grievances were meritless. It also does not entitle me to rewrite those losses as victories.

That distinction—between what I believe happened, what the documents establish, what a court actually held, and what the law permits me to infer—is the discipline this book ultimately required.

## E. SELECTED LEGAL AUTHORITIES

This list is a reader's guide, not an exhaustive table of authorities.

**Retaliatory arrest:** *Nieves v. Bartlett*, 587 U.S. 391 (2019); *Gonzalez v. Trevino*, 602 U.S. 653 (2024).  
**Right to record:** *Turner v. Driver*, 848 F.3d 678 (5th Cir. 2017).  
**Qualified immunity:** *Harlow v. Fitzgerald*, 457 U.S. 800 (1982); *Pearson v. Callahan*, 555 U.S. 223 (2009); *Hope v. Pelzer*, 536 U.S. 730 (2002).  
**Municipal liability / state action:** *Monell v. Department of Social Services*, 436 U.S. 658 (1978); *Lugar v. Edmondson Oil Co.*, 457 U.S. 922 (1982); *West v. Atkins*, 487 U.S. 42 (1988); *Will v. Michigan Department of State Police*, 491 U.S. 58 (1989).  
**Judicial / prosecutorial immunity:** *Stump v. Sparkman*, 435 U.S. 349 (1978); *Mireles v. Waco*, 502 U.S. 9 (1991); *Imbler v. Pachtman*, 424 U.S. 409 (1976); *Buckley v. Fitzsimmons*, 509 U.S. 259 (1993); *Kalina v. Fletcher*, 522 U.S. 118 (1997).  
**Contempt and impartial adjudication:** *In re Murchison*, 349 U.S. 133 (1955); *Mayberry v. Pennsylvania*, 400 U.S. 455 (1971).  
**Blood-warrant affidavit:** *Franks v. Delaware*, 438 U.S. 154 (1978); *Missouri v. McNeely*, 569 U.S. 141 (2013); *Birchfield v. North Dakota*, 579 U.S. 438 (2016).  
**Preservation / disclosure:** *Brady v. Maryland*, 373 U.S. 83 (1963); *California v. Trombetta*, 467 U.S. 479 (1984); *Arizona v. Youngblood*, 488 U.S. 51 (1988).  
**Revocation process:** *Morrissey v. Brewer*, 408 U.S. 471 (1972); *Gagnon v. Scarpelli*, 411 U.S. 778 (1973); Miss. Code Ann. § 47-7-37.  
**Favorable termination:** *Thompson v. Clark*, 596 U.S. 36 (2022).  
**Release-dismissal agreements:** *Town of Newton v. Rumery*, 480 U.S. 386 (1987).  
**Constitutional speedy trial:** *Barker v. Wingo*, 407 U.S. 514 (1972).

Earlier drafts and filings also invoked legal theories that this edition withdraws. In particular, Section 802 of the USA PATRIOT Act is not a civil cause of action against a county sheriff, and 26 U.S.C. § 7214 concerns federal revenue officers rather than Mississippi county officials. Those theories are not relied upon here.

## F. SOURCE GAPS AND DELIBERATE LIMITS

The following are genuine gaps or publication limits. They should not be confused with documents that have now been located.

1. **The original pre-arrest affidavit, complaint, or warrant application underlying the May 26, 2017 Lynch arrest has not been located in the present archive.** The July 6 Criminal Information and plea transcript independently establish the later "on or before May 8, 2017" allegation. The book therefore does not attribute that May 8 allegation to the May 26 arrest warrant unless the pre-arrest instrument is produced.
2. **The signed Texas blood-warrant instrument itself has not been located in the present archive.** Osteen's affidavit/application and his later sworn testimony establish that a judge signed a blood warrant; the book therefore does not characterize the blood draw as warrantless.
3. The project does not possess the FBI 302 from the May 25, 2017 meeting.
4. Some audio/video sources survive only through published or archived copies and machine transcripts. Where exact wording matters, official transcripts or direct audio verification take precedence over ASR.
5. Several recordings or camera angles requested in earlier proceedings were not produced or have not been located, including portions of the December 2020 and February 2021 video record. Their absence does not establish what they would have shown.
6. Communicare's precise legal status for federal state-action purposes remains unresolved in the source archive.
7. The sealed portions of the 2025–2026 federal criminal record are intentionally not reproduced or narrated beyond what the public docket and permitted sources establish.
8. The book does not claim that an unidentified source, a missing recording, or an unproduced document proves a conspiracy merely because the record is incomplete.

The following documents were once listed as missing but have now been located and are **not** source gaps: the July 2017 plea transcript, plea petition, signed covenant not to sue, and Major John Hill's May 30, 2017 Sheriff's Office report.

---

## G. RECORDING AND TRANSCRIPTION METHOD

The manuscript uses contemporaneous recordings extensively. Their evidentiary value depends on how they are handled.

- An official or certified court transcript controls over an automatic transcript of the same proceeding.
- A direct recording controls over later recollection where the two conflict.
- Automatic speech recognition is treated as a search and drafting aid. It is not treated as verbatim merely because software produced quotation marks around it.
- Proper names, dates, and legally significant quotations are checked against the underlying recording or another primary source whenever possible.
- A gap in a recording is described as a gap. A camera that did not cover an event is not described as having "lost" footage of that event.
- A document generated after an arrest is not described as the pre-arrest probable-cause instrument unless the record establishes that it served that function.

## I. STANDING CORRECTIONS FROM EARLIER EDITIONS

This section records claims that earlier drafts made too strongly or incorrectly. They remain listed because silent correction would defeat the archival purpose of the book.

1. **Rachel Alcorn did not identify Sheriff Joey East by name as her source.** The recorded call refers to local law enforcement. Any attribution to a particular officer requires separate evidence.
2. **The November 2022 revocation cannot accurately be summarized as "Beavers committed perjury and that sent me to prison."** Her testimony contained disputed and hearsay-based assertions, but she also confirmed that Reardon called the Sheriff's Department in advance, and the court's ruling rested on its interpretation of the banishment condition and Reardon's presence.
3. **Reardon was not simply denied counsel at the 2022 revocation hearing.** The transcript shows a waiver of counsel after questioning by the court.
4. **No evidence connects a docket-assignment irregularity to ballot tampering.** That inference is withdrawn.
5. **The USA PATRIOT Act domestic-terrorism provision and 26 U.S.C. § 7214 theories previously asserted against county officials were legally misplaced.** They are withdrawn.
6. **Emails following the 2017 Marine Corps records dispute do not establish implied threats merely because their timing was suspicious to the author.** That characterization is withdrawn absent direct evidence.
7. **The author did not know PFC Bradley Foust personally.** The Prologue now separates the author's recollection of the training environment from Jay Andrews's independent affidavit and the Marine Corps records.
8. **Communicare is not described as a proven governmental or quasi-governmental entity.** Its federal state-action status remains an unresolved legal and factual question.
9. **The January 21, 2025 PCR order did not vacate the 2022 aggravated-stalking conviction.** It vacated the 2024 two-year revocation sentence, resentenced the technical violation to 120 days, ordered release, and reinstated the original probation/banishment terms.
10. **The February 24, 2026 Mississippi Court of Appeals decision did not reverse the underlying Justice Court convictions.** It reversed the Circuit Court's dismissal of the appeal and remanded.
11. **The New Orleans City Hall case does not contain proof that RTCC footage of the altercation was destroyed or "lost."** The produced camera material did not capture the altercation itself.
12. **Magistrate Judge Edison's statement that Osteen "lied to Judge Tollison" is not a criminal adjudication of perjury.** The manuscript uses the judge's actual word and no stronger one.
13. **Edison's unreasonable-seizure discussion was not a final merits judgment for Reardon.** The R&R said the complaint stated a constitutional claim, then recommended qualified immunity; the district court adopted the recommendation and entered judgment against Reardon.
14. **The federal record does not establish that Circuit Clerk Jeff Busby personally transmitted the March 25, 2024 letter to Texas.** The R&R relied on evidence that Assistant District Attorney Steven Jubera transmitted it. Earlier claims of literal falsity in Busby's affidavit are withdrawn to the extent they depended on Busby personally sending the letter.
15. **The August 7, 2026 federal sentence should not be described simply as "time served plus probation."** The judge clarified that Reardon was placed on two years of unsupervised probation and that prior custody would be credited if probation were later revoked and resentencing occurred.
16. **The "on or before May 8, 2017" AR-15 allegation is documented, but not in a pre-arrest affidavit presently in the archive.** It appears in the July 6 Criminal Information and plea record. Earlier versions wrongly treated it as a fact sworn into the May 26 arrest warrant.
17. **Major John Hill's May 30, 2017 report has been located.** It records Reardon's profanity, Rhea Tannehill approaching with a water bottle and saying "I'LL BASH YOUR HEAD IN," and Hill stepping in front of Rhea and telling him to leave. It does not say Hill physically restrained Rhea or that an assault occurred.
18. **Brennan Horan and Rhea Tannehill are not established to have attended law school together.** A June 27, 2020 message from attorney Christi McCoy states that Brennan graduated roughly ten years before Rhea and that they were not in law school together. The contrary claim is withdrawn.
19. **The Galveston toxicology did not establish "zero drugs."** It reported no alcohol and separately reported methamphetamine at 0.13 mg/L. The author disputes the significance and reliability of that result, but the laboratory result itself is not omitted.
20. **The roughly eleven months of custody following the August 2023 Galveston arrest cannot be attributed solely to Osteen's affidavit or a single Texas bond.** Texas and Mississippi proceedings, including the Mississippi revocation hold, overlapped.

---

## J. SOURCE HIERARCHY USED FOR THIS EDITION

When sources conflict, this edition uses the following order of confidence:

1. Signed court orders, certified transcripts, filed pleadings, official agency records, laboratory reports, and authenticated public-record productions.
2. Contemporaneous audio/video recordings and contemporaneous written communications.
3. Later sworn statements and later recorded recollections, identified as such.
4. The author's unsworn recollection where no stronger source exists.
5. Secondary summaries, prior manuscript drafts, AI-generated analyses, news coverage, and internal timelines as research leads—not as substitutes for the underlying record.

A later document can be more complete without being more trustworthy. A recent upload date does not make an old summary authoritative. Where the book cannot resolve a conflict from the source record, the conflict is left visible rather than silently reconciled.
